<a id='question-048'></a>

### Question 048: Joint and Soft Tissue Pathology: Focus 01-48 (nodules)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating subcutaneous nodules based on histopathology, specifically recognizing the features of rheumatoid nodules versus other granulomatous or neoplastic processes. (鉴别皮下结节的组织病理学特征，特别是区分类风湿结节与其他肉芽肿或肿瘤过程。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old female with a 20-year history of rheumatoid arthritis (RA), currently treated with methotrexate and hydroxychloroquine, presents with a new, firm, non-tender nodule on her left elbow. She denies fever, chills, or weight loss. Physical examination reveals multiple subcutaneous nodules, ranging from 1 to 3 cm in diameter, over pressure points (elbows, forearms, occiput). Her rheumatoid factor (RF) and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide (anti-CCP) antibodies are positive. A biopsy of the elbow nodule is performed. Histopathology shows a central area of fibrinoid necrosis surrounded by palisading histiocytes and multinucleated giant cells. Scattered lymphocytes are also present. Special stains for microorganisms are negative.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying pathological mechanism responsible for the formation of this nodule?

(A) Immune complex deposition leading to vasculitis and subsequent necrosis.
(B) Granulomatous inflammation driven by activated macrophages responding to an infectious agent.
(C) Epithelioid histiocyte proliferation with central caseous necrosis characteristic of tuberculosis.
(D) Neoplastic proliferation of spindle cells with a storiform pattern, often associated with chronic inflammation.
(E) Fibrinoid necrosis surrounded by palisading histiocytes and multinucleated giant cells, typically found in subcutaneous tissue in patients with long-standing RA.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old female with long-standing (20 years) rheumatoid arthritis (RA). This immediately points towards complications associated with chronic RA.
- **Presentation:** New, firm, non-tender nodule on the elbow, a common pressure point. Multiple nodules over pressure points (elbows, forearms, occiput) are characteristic. Absence of systemic symptoms (fever, chills, weight loss) makes systemic infection less likely.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Positive RF and anti-CCP antibodies confirm the diagnosis of RA.
- **Biopsy Findings:** The key finding is the histopathology: "central area of fibrinoid necrosis surrounded by palisading histiocytes and multinucleated giant cells." This specific description is the hallmark of a rheumatoid nodule. Negative stains for microorganisms rule out infectious causes.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Rheumatoid Nodules:** These are subcutaneous lesions occurring in about 20-30% of patients with long-standing, seropositive RA (positive RF and/or anti-CCP).
- **Location:** Typically found over pressure points (olecranon, metacarpophalangeal joints, Achilles tendon, occiput).
- **Histopathology:** Characterized by a central core of fibrinoid necrosis, often containing degenerated collagen and inflammatory cells. This core is surrounded by a layer of palisading histiocytes (macrophages) and multinucleated giant cells. Lymphocytes are also present in the periphery.
- **Pathogenesis:** The exact pathogenesis is not fully understood, but it is believed to involve immune complex deposition in small blood vessels, leading to vasculitis, fibrinoid necrosis, and subsequent inflammation. The palisading histiocytes are thought to be activated macrophages attempting to contain the inflammatory process. The presence of RF and anti-CCP antibodies suggests an immune-mediated process. The location over pressure points suggests mechanical stress may play a role.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Immune complex deposition leading to vasculitis and subsequent necrosis. While immune complex deposition and vasculitis are part of the pathogenesis of rheumatoid nodules, this option is less specific than (E). Option (E) describes the *specific histological appearance* of the nodule, which is the most direct answer to the question based on the biopsy findings. This option describes the *process* leading to the nodule, not the specific histological hallmark.
- (B): Granulomatous inflammation driven by activated macrophages responding to an infectious agent. This describes a typical infectious granuloma (e.g., tuberculosis, fungal infection). While rheumatoid nodules contain macrophages and giant cells, the central fibrinoid necrosis and the clinical context (long-standing RA, negative infectious stains) make an infectious etiology unlikely. The granulomas in rheumatoid nodules are considered a form of vasculitis-associated inflammation rather than a response to an infectious agent.
- (C): Epithelioid histiocyte proliferation with central caseous necrosis characteristic of tuberculosis. This describes caseating granulomas, typical of tuberculosis. Rheumatoid nodules lack caseous necrosis; they have fibrinoid necrosis. The histiocytes in rheumatoid nodules are palisading rather than forming a well-defined epithelioid granuloma.
- (D): Neoplastic proliferation of spindle cells with a storiform pattern, often associated with chronic inflammation. This describes dermatofibrosarcoma protuberans (DFSP) or other spindle cell neoplasms. While these can occur in the skin, the specific histopathology (fibrinoid necrosis, palisading histiocytes) is not characteristic of these neoplasms. DFSP typically has a storiform pattern and lacks significant necrosis or palisading.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Rheumatoid Nodule (Correct Answer) | Infectious Granuloma (e.g., TB) | Vasculitis (e.g., Polyarteritis Nodosa) | Spindle Cell Neoplasm (e.g., DFSP) |
|--------------------------|--------------------------------------|---------------------------------|----------------------------------------|------------------------------------|
| **Clinical Context**     | Long-standing RA, seropositive       | Exposure, systemic symptoms     | Systemic vasculitic symptoms           | Usually solitary, slow-growing     |
| **Location**             | Pressure points                      | Variable                         | Variable, often systemic               | Variable                           |
| **Histopathology**       | Fibrinoid necrosis, palisading histiocytes, giant cells | Caseous necrosis, epithelioid histiocytes, Langhans giant cells | Fibrinoid necrosis, vessel wall inflammation | Spindle cells, storiform pattern |
| **Necrosis Type**        | Fibrinoid                            | Caseous                          | Fibrinoid                              | Variable, often absent             |
| **Key Cells**            | Palisading histiocytes, giant cells  | Epithelioid histiocytes, Langhans giant cells | Inflammatory cells around vessels | Spindle cells                     |
| **Microbial Stains**     | Negative                             | Positive (if infectious)         | Negative                                | Negative                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Rheumatoid nodules are a specific complication of long-standing, seropositive rheumatoid arthritis. Their characteristic histopathology involves central fibrinoid necrosis surrounded by palisading histiocytes and multinucleated giant cells. This pattern is distinct from infectious granulomas (caseous necrosis), other forms of vasculitis, and neoplastic processes. Key differentials include infectious granulomas (TB, fungi), other vasculitides, and skin neoplasms.

<a id='question-049'></a>

### Question 049: Joint and Soft Tissue Pathology: Synovial
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the pathogenesis and histological features of pigmented villonodular synovitis (PVNS), specifically differentiating it from other synovial proliferative disorders based on cellular morphology and immunohistochemical markers. (考察色素性滑膜结节性滑膜炎 (PVNS) 的发病机制和组织学特征，特别是根据细胞形态和免疫组织化学标记与其他滑膜增生性疾病的鉴别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old female presents to the orthopedic clinic complaining of insidious onset of left knee pain, swelling, and stiffness over the past 6 months. She denies any history of trauma or systemic inflammatory disease. Physical examination reveals a moderate knee effusion and tenderness along the medial joint line. Radiographs show soft tissue swelling and bony erosions around the medial femoral condyle. MRI reveals a large, lobulated synovial mass extending into the intercondylar notch, with low signal intensity on T2-weighted images. Synovial biopsy is performed. Histopathology shows a proliferation of mononuclear stromal cells admixed with numerous hemosiderin-laden macrophages, giant cells, and reactive lymphoid cells. The stromal cells are positive for CD68 and negative for epithelial markers (cytokeratin) and melanoma markers (S100). Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism driving the proliferation of mononuclear stromal cells in this patient's synovial lesion?

(A) Somatic mutation in the *BRAF* gene leading to constitutive activation of the MAPK pathway.
(B) Chronic inflammation secondary to an underlying systemic autoimmune disease, such as rheumatoid arthritis.
(C) Aberrant expression of vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) leading to angiogenesis and synovial hyperplasia.
(D) Translocation involving the *IGH* locus and a *COL6A* gene, resulting in the fusion protein characteristic of tenosynovial giant cell tumor (TGCT).
(E) Viral infection, such as parvovirus B19, triggering an inflammatory response in the synovium.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old female, common demographic for PVNS.
- **Symptoms:** Insidious onset knee pain, swelling, stiffness (6 months) - typical presentation of PVNS.
- **History:** No trauma, no systemic inflammatory disease - helps rule out reactive arthritis or RA.
- **Physical Exam:** Knee effusion, medial joint line tenderness - consistent with intra-articular pathology.
- **Radiographs:** Soft tissue swelling, bony erosions - characteristic findings in PVNS.
- **MRI:** Lobulated synovial mass, low T2 signal (due to hemosiderin) - highly suggestive of PVNS.
- **Histopathology:** Proliferation of mononuclear stromal cells, hemosiderin-laden macrophages, giant cells, reactive lymphoid cells - classic features of PVNS.
- **Immunohistochemistry:** Stromal cells CD68+, CK-, S100- - confirms macrophage/histiocyte lineage and rules out epithelial or melanocytic origin.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying mechanism driving the proliferation of mononuclear stromal cells.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Pigmented Villonodular Synovitis (PVNS):** A rare, benign, proliferative disorder of the synovium characterized by synovial thickening, villous projections, and deposition of hemosiderin.
- **Pathogenesis:** The exact cause is unknown, but it is thought to be a reactive process, not a true neoplasm. It is characterized by excessive production of synovial fluid and proliferation of synovial lining cells and stromal cells (macrophages/histiocytes).
- **Key Feature:** The mononuclear stromal cells are the primary proliferating component. These cells are reactive histiocytes/macrophages.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** Aberrant expression and increased levels of Vascular Endothelial Growth Factor (VEGF) within the synovium are considered a key driver of the disease. VEGF promotes angiogenesis (formation of new blood vessels), which contributes to the synovial hyperplasia, effusion, and hemosiderin deposition (from red blood cell extravasation). The increased vascularity and leakage are hallmarks.
- **Histology:** Characterized by mononuclear stromal cell proliferation, hemosiderin deposition (giving the characteristic brown color), giant cells, and reactive lymphocytes. The stromal cells are CD68 positive (macrophage marker).
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The question requires differentiating PVNS from other synovial lesions.
    - TGCT (Option D): Characterized by giant cells and a specific genetic translocation *IGH-COL6A*.
    - Rheumatoid Arthritis (Option B): Systemic inflammatory disease with characteristic synovial inflammation (lymphocytes, plasma cells, pannus formation).
    - Melanoma (ruled out by S100 negativity).
    - BRAF mutation (Option A): Associated with TGCT, not typically PVNS.
    - Viral infection (Option E): Can cause transient arthritis, but not the chronic, proliferative lesion described.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Somatic mutation in the *BRAF* gene leading to constitutive activation of the MAPK pathway: This is the characteristic genetic alteration found in the majority of tenosynovial giant cell tumors (TGCTs), not PVNS. TGCTs also have a distinct histology with prominent giant cells and a specific translocation.
- (B) Chronic inflammation secondary to an underlying systemic autoimmune disease, such as rheumatoid arthritis: While chronic inflammation is present in PVNS, it is considered a localized reactive process, not typically associated with systemic autoimmune diseases like RA. RA histology shows a different inflammatory infiltrate (lymphocytes, plasma cells) and pannus formation. The patient history specifically denies systemic inflammatory disease.
- (D) Translocation involving the *IGH* locus and a *COL6A* gene, resulting in the fusion protein characteristic of tenosynovial giant cell tumor (TGCT): This is the defining genetic abnormality of TGCT, a distinct entity from PVNS. TGCT histology is characterized by numerous multinucleated giant cells and mononuclear stromal cells, but the genetic basis and typical location (tendons/synovium) differ.
- (E) Viral infection, such as parvovirus B19, triggering an inflammatory response in the synovium: Viral infections can cause acute or chronic arthritis, but they do not typically lead to the specific chronic, proliferative synovial lesion with hemosiderin deposition characteristic of PVNS.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Pigmented Villonodular Synovitis (PVNS) | Tenosynovial Giant Cell Tumor (TGCT) | Rheumatoid Arthritis (RA) |
|-------------------------|-----------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|---------------------------|
| **Primary Cell Type**   | Mononuclear stromal cells (histiocytes) | Multinucleated giant cells, stromal cells | Lymphocytes, plasma cells |
| **Hemosiderin**         | Prominent                               | Absent/Minimal                       | Absent                    |
| **Genetic Abnormality** | None identified (reactive process)      | *IGH-COL6A* translocation             | None specific             |
| **Key Molecular Factor**| VEGF overexpression                     | BRAF mutation (often)                | Cytokines (TNF-α, IL-1)    |
| **Location**            | Synovium (joint)                        | Tendon sheath, synovium              | Synovium                  |
| **Histology**           | Mononuclear cells, hemosiderin, giant cells | Giant cells, mononuclear cells       | Pannus, inflammation      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
PVNS is characterized by synovial proliferation driven by VEGF, leading to angiogenesis and hemosiderin deposition. Differentiate it from TGCT (giant cells, *IGH-COL6A* translocation, often *BRAF* mutation) and RA (systemic inflammation, pannus). The key histological features are mononuclear stromal cells and hemosiderin.

<a id='question-050'></a>

### Question 050: Joint and Soft Tissue Pathology: Focus 01-50 (proliferation)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular pathogenesis of synovial sarcoma, specifically the characteristic chromosomal translocation t(X;18)(p11;q11) leading to the SYT-SSX fusion gene, and its implications for diagnosis and prognosis. (理解滑膜肉瘤的分子发病机制，特别是特征性染色体易位t(X;18)(p11;q11)导致SYT-SSX融合基因，及其对诊断和预后的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the orthopedic clinic with a 6-month history of a slowly enlarging, painless mass located deep to the fascia near his left knee joint. He denies any history of trauma. Physical examination reveals a firm, non-tender, 5 cm mass with restricted mobility of the underlying joint. Radiographs show a soft tissue mass adjacent to the bone, without evidence of cortical destruction or periosteal reaction. MRI confirms a well-circumscribed, heterogeneous soft tissue mass with some cystic changes, abutting the posterior aspect of the distal femur. A core needle biopsy is performed. Histopathological examination reveals a biphasic tumor composed of spindle cells arranged in fascicles and epithelial-like cells forming glandular structures. Immunohistochemical staining shows the spindle cells positive for vimentin and the epithelial-like cells positive for cytokeratin and EMA. Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis is performed on the biopsy specimen.

Which of the following molecular findings is most characteristic of this patient's tumor?

(A) Amplification of the *MYC* oncogene on chromosome 8
(B) Translocation involving the *EWSR1* gene on chromosome 22
(C) Fusion of the *SYT* gene on chromosome 18 with an *SSX* gene on the X chromosome
(D) Mutation of the *TP53* tumor suppressor gene on chromosome 17
(E) Overexpression of the *MDM2* gene on chromosome 12

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male. Synovial sarcoma typically affects young adults (20-40 years old).
- **Presentation:** Slowly enlarging, painless mass near a joint (knee), deep to the fascia. This location (extremities, especially near joints) is characteristic of synovial sarcoma. Painlessness is common initially.
- **Physical Exam:** Firm, non-tender mass, restricted joint mobility. Consistent with a soft tissue tumor impinging on the joint.
- **Imaging:** Soft tissue mass abutting bone, no cortical destruction. Suggests a soft tissue origin, potentially aggressive but not overtly metastatic or bone-destroying at this stage.
- **Histopathology:** Biphasic tumor (spindle cells + epithelial-like cells forming glands). This is the classic histological pattern of synovial sarcoma, although monophasic variants exist.
- **Immunohistochemistry:** Spindle cells positive for vimentin (mesenchymal marker), epithelial-like cells positive for cytokeratin and EMA (epithelial markers). This confirms the biphasic nature and epithelial differentiation.
- **FISH Analysis:** The question asks for the characteristic molecular finding. The clinical and histological features strongly suggest synovial sarcoma.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- Synovial sarcoma is a malignant soft tissue tumor.
- Despite its name, it does not arise from synovial tissue.
- The characteristic genetic abnormality is a reciprocal translocation between chromosome X and chromosome 18, denoted as t(X;18)(p11;q11).
- This translocation results in the fusion of the *SYT* gene (located on 18q11) with one of the *SSX* genes (*SSX1*, *SSX2*, or *SSX4*) located on Xp11.
- The most common fusion is SYT-SSX1 (about 70% of cases), followed by SYT-SSX2 (about 20%) and SYT-SSX4 (about 10%).
- The SYT-SSX fusion protein acts as an aberrant transcription factor, driving cell proliferation and tumorigenesis.
- FISH analysis is a standard diagnostic tool used to detect this specific translocation in suspected cases of synovial sarcoma.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Amplification of the *MYC* oncogene on chromosome 8: *MYC* amplification is characteristic of Burkitt lymphoma and some other high-grade lymphomas, as well as certain neuroblastomas and small cell lung cancers. It is not associated with synovial sarcoma.
- (B) Translocation involving the *EWSR1* gene on chromosome 22: Translocations involving *EWSR1* are characteristic of Ewing sarcoma family of tumors (ESFT), typically t(11;22)(q24;q12) resulting in the *EWSR1-FLI1* fusion. This is a distinct entity from synovial sarcoma, usually affecting bone or soft tissue in children and young adults, with a different histology (small round blue cells).
- (D) Mutation of the *TP53* tumor suppressor gene on chromosome 17: *TP53* mutations are very common in many cancers, including Li-Fraumeni syndrome-associated tumors, and are frequently found in high-grade sarcomas like osteosarcoma and chondrosarcoma, as well as carcinomas. While *TP53* mutations can occur in synovial sarcoma, they are not the defining or characteristic molecular feature. The defining feature is the t(X;18) translocation.
- (E) Overexpression of the *MDM2* gene on chromosome 12: Amplification of the *MDM2* gene (located on 12q15) is characteristic of well-differentiated and anaplastic liposarcomas, leading to overexpression of the MDM2 protein, which inhibits p53. This is not associated with synovial sarcoma.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Synovial Sarcoma                                  | Ewing Sarcoma Family of Tumors (ESFT)          | Liposarcoma (Well-differentiated/Anaplastic) | Burkitt Lymphoma                       |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------|
| **Characteristic Gene Abnormality** | t(X;18)(p11;q11) -> *SYT-SSX* fusion          | t(11;22)(q24;q12) -> *EWSR1-FLI1* fusion        | Amplification of *MDM2* (chr 12q15)        | t(8;14)(q24;q32) -> *MYC* translocation |
| **Typical Age**       | Young adults (20-40)                             | Children/Young adults                          | Middle-aged/Older adults                     | Children/Young adults                  |
| **Typical Location**  | Extremities, near joints                         | Bone (diaphysis), soft tissue                   | Deep soft tissues (retroperitoneum, limbs)  | Jaw, abdomen, CNS                      |
| **Histology**         | Biphasic (spindle + epithelial) or Monophasic     | Small round blue cells                          | Lipoblasts (well-diff) / Anaplastic (poorly diff) | Lymphoblasts                            |
| **Key IHC Markers**   | Vimentin (+), Cytokeratin (+), EMA (+)            | CD99 (+), FLI1 (+)                               | S100 (+), Desmin (-)                         | CD20 (+), CD10 (+), MYC (+)            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Synovial sarcoma is characterized by the t(X;18) translocation leading to a SYT-SSX fusion gene. This molecular hallmark is crucial for diagnosis, often confirmed by FISH. Differentiating synovial sarcoma from other soft tissue tumors like Ewing sarcoma (EWSR1 translocation) or liposarcoma (MDM2 amplification) requires understanding their distinct genetic profiles and histological features. Remember the biphasic histology (spindle and epithelial cells) and location near joints for synovial sarcoma.
